  The core now runs the cache's checkpoints itself, and a failed checkpoint or a
  failed sync of the write-ahead log at a commit stops the instance for good.
  Production runs `synchronous = full`, as the product owner decided. Everything
  passes except where noted below. Branch `core-checkpoints`, last commit
  `3ceb9d6a` (main merged in; I resolved one conflict in
  `docs/todo/storage-core-tests.md`). Not pushed.

  - **Who and when:** `pragma wal_autocheckpoint = 0` is set on every
  connection, readers too. The writer, under the writer lock, runs `pragma
  wal_checkpoint(noop)` after each commit (after `Tx.commitCache`, and after
  each commit object of a catch-up). When the log holds 1000 frames or more, it
  runs a passive checkpoint. That is exactly when and how SQLite's automatic
  checkpoint ran, minus the dropped error. Close runs no checkpoint of the core;
  SQLite's own checkpoint at the last close keeps the log if it fails, and the
  next open recovers it. A spare never writes, so it never checkpoints.
  - **What stops and what doesn't:** a checkpoint held back by readers, busy
  because another connection checkpoints, or refused as `SQLITE_LOCKED` while a
  statement is open is no failure; the next commit checkpoints. Any other error
  stops the instance with `ErrLocal`, and the commit still returns nil
  (`committed_and_stopped`), since it is in the log.
  - **Unbounded WAL:** the log grows only by what is committed while the oldest
  read transaction is open, as under SQLite before. After the read ends, the
  next commit's checkpoint copies the whole log and the commit after it starts
  the log again. Restart or truncate checkpoints are not needed: they would make
  commits wait for readers and bound nothing more.
  - **Tests:** `TestDB_checkpoint` (8 subtests) and `TestDB_sync`, both using
  faultvfs, no real time. The test of the research note now stops at the first
  commit, which is still acknowledged. A restart finds every commit, and
  replaying into an empty cache gives the same rows. They pass with `-count=20`
  and with `-race -count=20`.
  - **Planted bug `ignoreCheckpointError`:** a new simulation cell runs with
  syncs on and a checkpoint every 4 frames, and fails a sync of the cache file
  or of the log, two faults per node. `run.close` requires at most one failed
  sync per open and a `SQLITE_IOERR_FSYNC` stop for it. At 1000 seeds, 681 seeds
  found the bug (first ones 2 3 5 6 8 9 11 12 13 14), with none of the control
  and none without the bug.
  - **Cost (Mac Mini, load 17 to 25, six alternating runs each):**

  | | `normal` | `full` |
  |---|---|---|
  | Commits of 10 rows, per second | 1916 / 2075 | 2091 / 1880 |
  | Catch-up of 3000 commits | 0.49 s / 0.50 s | 0.66 s / 0.57 s |

    First figure is on `/Volumes/990`, second on the main disk. No difference
  was significant. The core's checkpoints cost the same as SQLite's automatic
  ones: 570 µs against 561 µs per commit. Catch-up applies one cache transaction
  per commit object, so `full` costs one sync per commit object. Whether to
  batch catch-up is recorded as open, not built.

⏺ A research agent is on it, with primary sources only. It looks at how others
  learn whether a commit landed after an ambiguous answer, and how they keep two
  distinct transactions apart:

  - FoundationDB's unknown commit result and its idempotency IDs
  - Delta Lake, Iceberg, and similar table formats on object storage

  says what follows for (a), (b), (c), or a fourth option.

  The evaluator in `internal/expr` now computes `||`, `lower` and `hex`, and
  checks them against SQLite. The engine still refuses every statement with text
  before it reaches the evaluator. `make test`, `make lint`, `-race`, and
  `-count=20` of the new tests all pass. Branch `text-expr`, last commit
  `f52fac9e`, not pushed; worktree
  `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a2d3581f117d3114a`.

  1. **Types and refusals at `Check`/`Bind`.** New types `TypeText` and
  `TypeBlob`. `||` and `lower` take text; `hex` takes text or a blob (text is
  the hex of its UTF-8 bytes). Refused by column type at `Check` and by argument
  at `Bind`, each at its offset: numbers or blobs in `||`/`lower` ("column of
  type INT in a concatenation", "argument 1 in a call of lower, which is a
  blob"), and numbers in `hex`. A TEXT or BLOB column alone now has its column's
  type instead of being refused.
  2. **NULL, same as SQLite:** `NULL || 'a'` and `'a' || NULL` are NULL,
  `lower(NULL)` is NULL, `hex(NULL)` is `''`. A NULL argument gives `TypeNull`
  for `||` and `lower`, and `TypeText` for `hex`.
  3. **New error `expr.ErrTooLong`**, where SQLite gives `string or blob too
  big`:
     - `||` whose result is longer than the limit;
     - `lower` of a text as long as the limit or longer;
     - `hex` when 2n+1 exceeds the limit;
     - at `Bind`, a text or blob literal longer than the limit. For the blob
  literal this is stricter than SQLite, which reads it as NULL.

     `Bind(args, limit)` now takes the limit, clamped to 30..1e9 as SQLite does.
  `*expr.Error` has a new `Function` field. No other evaluation error can come
  from the three.
  4. **Allocations:** arithmetic still allocates nothing. A text operation
  allocates its result once, or not at all when it returns an operand or empty
  text. `'m_' || lower(hex(x))` costs 2, or 3 when the hex digits contain A–F.
  Values share bytes with the row; Go strings never change, so a caller can keep
  a value after the row is gone. `Value` grew from 16 to 32 bytes; the
  arithmetic benchmark could not be compared with `main` because machine load
  was 27.
  5. **Planted faults:** all 8 caught by both `TestDifferential` and the
  `FuzzExpr` seeds (non-ASCII fold, lower-case hex, swapped `||`, `hex(NULL)` as
  NULL, NULL `||` returning the other operand, each of the three limits off by
  one).
  6. **Fuzz run:** 5 minutes, 5.2M executions, no failure. I committed the 47
  corpus inputs that contain text as seeds.

› Message from general-purpose
  **Short answer:** I found no system that identifies a commit attempt only by
  its content and also promises a separate acknowledgement for each attempt. The
  systems that check after an ambiguous result whether their own commit landed
  all look for a value that only that attempt carries, and nearly all make it
  random: Delta Lake, Iceberg, SlateDB and FoundationDB use a random value, and
  Paimon uses a random ID per writer plus a sequence number. The systems with a
  content-derived identity are Git, Graft and ClickHouse's insert deduplication,
  and each of them treats equal content as one thing on purpose.

  **Systems (identity; where it is stored; what the writer does after an
  ambiguous result):**
  - **FoundationDB:** 16 random bytes; beside the data, expiring. A conflicting
  dummy commit, then a lookup of the ID. The feature is still marked
  experimental.
  - **Delta Lake:** a random UUID `txnId` in the commit file.
  `detectIdempotentSelfCommit` compares the winner's `txnId` with its own. That
  check is an internal option and is off by default.
  - **Iceberg:** a random UUID in each metadata file's name (snapshot IDs are
  random too). It checks whether its location is the current one or in
  `previousFiles`. This is the closest shape to `Tx.inLog`.
  - **Paimon:** a random UUID per writer plus a commit identifier, stored in
  each snapshot. It walks the snapshots looking for both.
  - **SlateDB:** a ULID drawn from its seeded `DbRand`, in the object's
  metadata. On `AlreadyExists` it reads the object's head and compares the ULID.
  - **Graft:** a content hash. An equal hash at the same position counts as
  success ("Someone (including us) pushed the same commit"). This is s4ql's
  mechanism.
  - **Kafka:** producer ID, epoch and sequence number, held by the broker and
  expiring after 7 days by default.
  - **DynamoDB:** a client token, kept for 10 minutes.
  - **CockroachDB:** a random transaction UUID, but the ambiguity goes to the
  client as `AmbiguousResultError` and is not resolved.
  - **Git:** a content hash with whole-second times. Two identical commits are
  one object, by intent.

  **Did anyone have this exact fault?** I found no report of the exact sequence.
  Graft has the same mechanism and treats it as benign, because for Graft a
  commit is a state, not an acknowledged event. ClickHouse has the fault class
  in plain form and documents it: separate inserts of the same rows are dropped
  as duplicates. Since 22.2 the fix is `insert_deduplication_token`, a token per
  insert.

  **What it means for the options:**
  - **(a) a random value in each commit's header:** this is what Delta, Iceberg,
  SlateDB and FoundationDB do. Their costs don't apply here: there is no
  separate ID store that has to expire and no reliance on object metadata. The
  value sits inside the immutable commit object that the log walk already reads.
  It works with deterministic simulation the way SlateDB does it: s4ql draws
  from `host.Uint64`, which is one seeded stream in the simulation and
  crypto/rand in production. It also leaves only one explanation for `ErrExists`
  on upload: the writer's own earlier attempt or a foreign object.
  - **(b) the instance ID in the header:** sound today, because the instance ID
  is already 128 random bits per process. But it only stays sound while an
  instance resolves an unknown outcome before it commits again. Paimon and Kafka
  add a sequence number for exactly that reason. It gains nothing over (a).
  - **(c) leave it and document it:** this leaves the lost update in place. A
  stopped or coarse clock can trigger it.
  - **Fourth option, a lease tenure (epoch) in the header:** it is (b) with a
  narrower scope. It would need a counter in the manifest that the lease doesn't
  have today, and it does nothing for this fault that (a) doesn't.
  - **What doesn't help:** a monotonic commit time in Delta's style, a sequence
  number taken from the parent, or comparing the existing object's checksum. Two
  writers on the same parent get equal values from all three.

  **Gaps:** Graft's plan for client provenance (`plans/grove.md`) is no longer
  on its main branch. Junio Hamano's git message and the etcd and Datomic claims
  rest on search excerpts, because the mail archives refused automated reads.
  Irmin, Noms, ZFS and WarpStream were not examined.

  **Commit:** `e1659103` on branch `research-commit-identity`, not pushed. It
  adds `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-ac8af594b4fc37c2
  b/docs/research/commit-identity.md` and its entry in `/Users/maragubot/Develop
  er/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-ac8af594b4fc37c2b/docs/research/README.md`.

⏺ Agent "Research commit identity in other systems" finished · 9m 22s

⏺ The research supports (a). The note is on main:
  docs/research/commit-identity.md.

  What others do. Every system found that checks "did my own commit land?" after
  an ambiguous answer looks for a value that only that attempt carries, and
  nearly all make it random:

  - Delta Lake: a random UUID in each commit file, compared with the winner's.
  - Iceberg: a random UUID in each metadata file's name. The closest shape to
    what we do.
  - SlateDB: a random ID drawn from a seeded generator, so it stays
    deterministic in simulation, which is what we would do too.
  - FoundationDB: 16 random bytes per transaction, kept beside the data.
  - Paimon and Kafka: a writer ID plus a sequence number.

  Who identifies by content alone. Git, Graft, and ClickHouse's insert
  deduplication, and each treats equal content as one thing on purpose. Graft
  has exactly our mechanism and calls a match success ("someone, including us,
  pushed the same commit"), which is right for it because a commit there is a
  state, not an acknowledged event. ClickHouse had our kind of fault in plain
  form, separate inserts of the same rows dropped as duplicates, and fixed it
  with a token per insert.

  Our exact fault: no report of it found anywhere.

  For the options:
  - (a) is the common practice, and the costs others pay for it (a separate
    store of IDs that must expire) do not apply to us, because the value sits
    inside the commit object we already read.
  - (b) gains nothing over (a), and stays sound only while an instance resolves
    an unknown outcome before it commits again.
  - (c) leaves the lost update in place.

  Some sources could not be read directly and are marked so in the note.

  Do you want to settle it with (a)?
› Message from general-purpose
  Review of `core-checkpoints` (worktree reset to it, branch renamed to
  `core-checkpoints-review`). The worktree is clean and the scratch directory is
  deleted. I found no fault that a test run shows. The one fault in s4ql is on
  paper, at close after a stop. There are three gaps in the tests.

  **Findings, most important first**
  1. **Fault in s4ql (on paper, and worse than the todo file says): the
  checkpoint at close after a stop can delete the only durable copy of pages.**
  A passive checkpoint that a read holds back copies frames into the cache file
  and moves `nBackfill` forward without syncing the file. The sync only happens
  when `mxSafeFrame==mxFrame` (`internal/sqlite/c/sqlite3.c` ~69283-69292).
     - What happens at close after a stop: `DB.Close` closes the readers first
  and then the writer (`closeIdleLocked`, `core.go` ~838). The last connection
  takes EXCLUSIVE and runs a passive checkpoint from `nBackfill` with its own
  sync flags. On success it deletes the WAL and the shm (`sqlite3WalClose`,
  69482-69517). `sqlite3PagerClose` drops the result (63207), so `c.close()`
  never sees it.
     - Pages that an earlier held-back checkpoint copied are not written again
  by that retry. Suppose their writeback failed and was reported by the failed
  checkpoint's fsync (Linux reports it once per fd). Then the retry's fsync can
  succeed and the WAL is deleted, while the file on disk holds old pages. After
  an eviction or a power loss, the cache serves wrong rows under a position that
  says it is current.
     - A spare on the same cache directory that takes the lease without
  reopening continues from the same `nBackfill`, so it has the same exposure. A
  fresh open is safe, because WAL recovery sets `nBackfill=0` (68483).
     - The open item in `docs/todo/cache-checkpoints.md` names the WAL's pages.
  Under `full` those are synced at every commit, so the real exposure is pages
  of the cache file. It needs a sync failure, a held-back checkpoint and a
  crash, but it is silent. The remedy is still
  `SQLITE_DBCONFIG_NO_CKPT_ON_CLOSE` after a stop.
  2. **"A failed sync surfaces at the commit that caused it":**
     - It is true for the WAL. `walFrames` (~71151) syncs before it appends to
  the wal-index, and a failure fails the commit, so `TestDB_sync` is right.
     - It is not true for the cache file. That file is synced only by a
  checkpoint that copies the whole log, so the failure surfaces at a later
  commit's checkpoint. Writes from held-back checkpoints are never synced on
  their own.
     - The comment at `cache.go` ~270 ("so a sync that fails fails the commit
  that needed it") reads as covering both files. It is wrong for the cache file.
  3. **Coverage of the paths.** Every commit to the cache is checkpointed:
  `Tx.Commit` (`tx.go` 1434), `catchUp` (`catchup.go` 95), and `setUp`'s first
  commit, which is small and fails the open. `wal_autocheckpoint = 0` is set in
  `openConn`, which both bindings and every later reader (`read.go:55`) go
  through. The browser's SQLite is also 3.54.1 and knows `noop`.
     - Readers run SQLite's default sync, FULL, also when `syncOff` is set, so a
  reader that closes last syncs anyway. It does no harm.
  4. **A stop where nothing failed: I found none.**
     - Every BUSY variant (primary code 5) and every LOCKED variant (6) counts
  as busy.
     - The checkpoint steps with plain `Step`, so a cancelled context cannot
  give `SQLITE_INTERRUPT`. `StepContext` installs its progress handler only for
  its own step.
     - An empty or small WAL returns before any checkpoint. A spare or a
  read-only instance commits nothing, so it never checkpoints. A reader in the
  same process gives `held_back`, not an error.
     - The one real but harmless case: the simulation's `CloseCache` closes a
  connection that is in use, so a checkpoint would fail with MISUSE. That
  instance has crashed anyway.
     - Minor: a caller of the core who always commits from inside a scan never
  checkpoints, so the WAL grows without bound. The engine cannot do this,
  because it refuses a commit while rows are read (`engine/tx.go` 212).
  5. **Q3: `committed_and_stopped` returning nil is correct.** The spec says a
  commit is acknowledged once the swap succeeds, and the instance stops if the
  cache then fails. The nil passes unchanged through `engine/tx.go` 219 and
  `engine.go` 589, which builds the RETURNING rows before the commit, and
  through `tx.go` 91 in the root package. The next call gets `ErrStopped`.
  6. **Test gap:** a busy row (`after.busy`, another connection holds the
  checkpoint lock) is never tested. Planted fault F6 below survived.
  7. **Telemetry:** a busy event from an error sets `err` to nil
  (`checkpoint.go` ~68), so the event does not say whether it was BUSY or
  LOCKED. It is minor. I found no timing dependence or skips in the new tests.

  **Planted faults** (each run with the named `-run` set, and once with more
  filters added; each reverted afterwards)
  - F1, catch-up's checkpoint error dropped: **caught**. Caught by the catch-up
  subtest of `TestDB_checkpoint`.
    - My first version, which dropped the return value only, survived and is not
  a valid fault, because `d.stop` runs inside `checkpoint`.
  - F2, threshold never reached: **caught** by 7 subtests.
  - F3, `wal_autocheckpoint` turned off only on the writer: **caught**.
  - F4, `synchronous = normal`: **caught** by both `TestDB_sync` subtests.
  - F5, LOCKED treated as a failure: **caught** by the scan-loop subtest.
  - F6, a busy row treated as a failure: **survived**.
  - F7, held-back treated as a failure: **caught**.
  - F8, stop not made permanent: **caught** by 3 subtests.
  - F9, a failed `noop` treated as below the threshold: **caught**.
  - F10, `committed_and_stopped` not set in `Tx.Commit`: **caught**.
  - The simulation cell caught none of these on its own. The unit tests carry
  them.

  **Q5, cost**
  - These open instances with syncs on: the core's scenario tests (`w.serve` and
  `w.open` with `core.Options{}`), the engine's `TestDifferential`,
  `TestSimulation` and the other engine tests (`engine/simulation_test.go` 514,
  `differential_test.go` 634), and the root package's tests (`conn.go` 96).
  - None of the engine or root tests fails syncs, and their crashes keep the
  operating system's cache, so syncs off there would weaken no check. The `full`
  path stays covered by `TestDB_sync`, `TestDB_checkpoint`, the sync test at
  `core_test.go` 1331, `cache_test.go` 810 and 837, and the simulation's `syncs`
  cell.
  - The obstacle: `syncOff` can only be set from the core's own tests
  (`export_test.go` 63), so the engine and root tests would need a hook exported
  for tests.
  - In the core, `newWorld` could default to `SyncOff`, with `SyncOn`, which
  already exists, in the tests that fail syncs.

  **Not checked:** I did not repeat the builder's long runs or measure anything
  on Linux. I did not show finding 1 with a VFS that keeps volatile and durable
  state apart. I did not review the simulation cell's checks line by line, and
  did not run the browser build.

⏺ Agent "Review core checkpoints" finished · 16m 19s

⏺ The review of the cache's checkpoints is in, and a builder is closing its
  points and landing the branch. Nothing here needs you now; the commit-header
  question above is still the one open decision.

  What the review found:

  - No stop where nothing failed, and 9 of 10 planted faults caught.
  - One fault on paper, at close after a stop. When an instance has stopped for
    a failed sync, closing it still lets SQLite checkpoint once more and delete
    the WAL. That is the "try again in place" our spec rules out, and it can
    throw away the only good copy of some pages. The fix is to close without a
    checkpoint after a stop, so the WAL stays for the next start. Being built.
  - A precision about full. A failed sync surfaces at the commit that caused it
    for the WAL, but for the cache file only at a later checkpoint. Comments and
    notes are corrected to say so.
  - A cost I had not thought through. With full, every test that opens an
    instance now syncs at each commit, also the thousands of test instances that
    never test syncing. On Linux in CI that is real work for no value. The
    builder makes tests run with syncs off unless they test syncing, with a test
    per layer that proves production still runs full, and reports CI job times
    before and after.

  What will come to you later, once it is reproduced: a second instance sharing
  the cache directory could still reset the WAL after the first one stopped. The
  remedy would be to mark such a cache as suspect and rebuild it from the log
  on the next start, which the spec today says we do not do.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  I've finished the statement pass for upserts on branch `upsert-stmt`; the last
  commit is 5f334b03, not pushed. `make test` and `make lint` pass, and so did
  the 400-sequence `TestDifferential` (1150 s) and five minutes of fuzzing
  (`FuzzCheck` 3 min, `FuzzSplit` and `FuzzGenerate` 1 min each). The new tests
  also pass with `-count=20` and `-race`.

  **Typed form.** `stmt.Insert.Conflict` is a `stmt.Conflict` with fields `Span`
  (with `Present()`), `Target []Name` with offsets, `Action` (`DoNothing` or
  `DoUpdate`), `Set []Assignment` and `Where []Condition`. `excluded.c` is a new
  node kind, `ExprExcluded`, not a flag on `ExprColumn`, so old code can't
  misread it as a table column. It carries a new field `Expression.Table`, the
  qualifier as written, because SQLite prints it: `no such column: EXCLUDED.zz`.
  `HasColumn` is true for it, and `excluded.c is null` is the one null test
  that has an `Expression`. Parameters are numbered VALUES, then SET, then
  WHERE. SQLite counts `excluded.c` as 2 deep, and the depth edges are checked
  against it.

  **Refusals, each against what SQLite does** (all of these SQLite accepts
  unless noted):
  - No target, refused at DO. SQLite applies it to every uniqueness constraint;
  I refused it because the agreed form names the key.
  - Target with WHERE: SQLite matches the primary key anyway.
  - Target with ASC/DESC (SQLite ignores the order), NULLS FIRST (SQLite
  errors), COLLATE (SQLite matches by collation), parentheses, an expression or
  literal (matches nothing), TRUE, `t.k`, `excluded.k` (SQLite errors), or a
  column twice.
  - A second ON CONFLICT clause: SQLite uses the first one whose target matches.
  - An assignment to a target column: SQLite moves the row and gives UNIQUE if
  the new key is taken. The target is always the key in s4ql, so this refuses
  every key change in DO UPDATE.
  - A subquery in DO UPDATE SET or WHERE.
  - `t.v` in DO UPDATE, and `excluded.v` in an upsert into a table named
  `excluded`, where SQLite reads it as the table's own column (I measured 101
  where 105 would be the excluded reading).
  - `excluded` with a schema, or in single quotes, brackets or backticks.
  - Everything an update already refuses in SET and WHERE keeps its name.
  REPLACE and INSERT OR keep their old messages.
  - Syntax errors (`excluded.*`, `set excluded.v`, ON CONFLICT on an update)
  come through as SQLite gives them.
  - I did not limit DO UPDATE to 2,000 assignments, because SQLite counts only
  an update's set list.

  **Left to the engine step, because it needs the table:**
  - Whether the target is the primary key; otherwise SQLite gives `does not
  match any PRIMARY KEY or UNIQUE constraint`.
  - Missing columns, in SQLite's order: SET is checked assignment by assignment,
  value before column.
  - Rows and counts, the DEFAULT behind an excluded column the list leaves out,
  exact types, NOT NULL, and query_only.

  **Outside `internal/sql/stmt`:**
  - `internal/engine/upsert.go` refuses an upsert as `ON CONFLICT clause` at its
  ON, called first in `textRefusal`, so before the database is read.
  - `engine.NewPlan` panics on an insert with a conflict clause, so nothing can
  plan one as a plain insert.
  - New tests in `internal/engine/upsert_test.go`.
  - An upsert rule in the engine's differential pair, placed before its
  length-limit rule, plus two `FuzzSQL` seeds.
  - The same rule, and a `TestPair` subtest, in
  `internal/sqlite/crosscheck/drivercheck/drivercheck_test.go`.

  **Existing fault, found by a reviewer, not fixed:** on `main`, `update t1 set
  v1 = 1 where lower(v1) = 'a'` run through `callPrepareExec` fails the driver's
  differential pair. Its `checkRefused` rejects a refusal at prepare, and
  Prepare refuses text expressions there. The README Results table had also been
  stale since text functions; I updated it.

  **For Markus:** the list above of forms SQLite accepts and s4ql now refuses.
  `TestAllowList` now allows `ColumnRef.Table` for every statement; the error
  cases still guard `t.v`.

  Everything is recorded in `/Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/a
  gent-a738805632c424ac0/docs/todo/upserts.md`, the "Upserts" section of
  `internal/sql/stmt/README.md`, and the diary at
  `docs/diary/2026-11-26-upsert-stmt.md`.

⏺ The statement pass for upserts is built and is being landed. s4ql now reads
  insert ... on conflict (key) do nothing and do update set ..., with
  excluded.<column> for the proposed row, and still refuses to run them until
  the engine step is built.

  One design-flavoured detail, for your information: SQLite accepts several
  forms that s4ql refuses here, because the agreed form names the key and we do
  not copy leniencies:

  - on conflict without a target (SQLite applies it to every uniqueness
    constraint);
  - a second on conflict clause;
  - do update changing a column of the key (SQLite moves the row);
  - a subquery inside the clause.

  Say so if you want any of these supported; otherwise they stay refused.

  fine.

  Pull request #176 (branch `mutation-ci`, head 4a7aab00, with main merged in)
  has all 29 checks passing and is ready for you to merge locally. The workflow
  has not run yet, because it has to be on `main` first, so step 4's two CI runs

  - **What changed after the brief:** the coordinator first moved the round to
  every night, then the product owner's message replaced that. The workflow now
  has no schedule: you start it by hand with inputs `since`, `pkgs`, `run`,
  `sample`, `seed`, `shards` and `issue`. AGENTS.md on `main` already says the
  same.
  - **Split and merge:** `-shard i/n` deals the mutants out in key order, with
  the ones in `equivalent.txt` first. So the shards are disjoint, together
  complete, and the same on every machine, and each gets its share of every file
  within one. Split by measured times would differ between runners, so I didn't
  use them. `-merge PATTERN` refuses a shard of other code (another
  fingerprint), a mutant with results in two shards, a result outside the
  selection, and an output folder that already has results. It drops a last line
  that a killed shard cut short.
  - **What a round covers:** every declaration of `-pkgs all` that changed since
  the last recorded round. That is the commit in
  `internal/sql/mutation/round.txt`, which a builder writes after working
  through the round's issue. Or it is a later whole default round on `main` that
  left no work: that run uploads an artifact `mutation-round`, and the next run
  finds it through the API and checks it sits between `round.txt` and its own
  commit. `round.txt` starts at 8c53a53f (#156), so the next round is 2,208
  mutants: several statements in one `Exec` (#157–#168) and #172.
  - **Shards and concurrency:** GitHub's limits page gives 60 concurrent jobs on
  Team, and `gh api` shows maragudk is on Team. A public repo's `ubuntu-latest`
  runner has 4 CPUs, 16 GB and 14 GB of disk. One shard per 150 mutants, at
  most 6 by default, which leaves room for a pull request's CI (29 jobs) and the
  nightly (15). Each shard runs 3 mutants at once with 3 GiB of memory per test
  binary.
  - **Timeout rule:** each shard plans on its own runner, timing every stage
  alone. A stage's limit is 20 times its baseline, at least 1 minute. Timeouts
  count as killed and are listed separately by `file:line` so they can be rerun.
  - **Issue:** one open issue labelled `mutation`. Each run replaces its body
  with the latest report and adds a comment. The title names the commit the
  round counts from. A cancelled run, or one with nothing changed, opens
  nothing.
  - **Sample and cost:** `-sample f -seed s` draws a seeded fraction. The
  summary scales the unexplained survivors up in proportion; with none found
  among n mutants it gives an upper bound of 3/n at 95% (the rule of three). It
  says it cannot clear mutants outside the sample. Every summary shows
  runner-minutes, mutants per runner-minute, survivors found, setup and planning
  time per shard, and where the workers' time went (by layer, and the
  survivors' share).
  - **What I measured:** the last recorded round's `results.jsonl` was never
  committed and lived outside my worktree, so I couldn't use it. On the 15
  mutants of `filelimit_unix.go`, builds took 26 of 41 s for killed mutants and
  44 of 98 s for survivors. Mutants that don't compile cost 0.2–0.3 s each.
  Compiling the package before the test binary saved nothing and cost 17% more
  (164.7 s against 140.5 s), so I removed it.
  - **Not measured:** a local sample of the next round planned the engine's
  first stage at 5 min 40 s under a load of 32, so I stopped it. The CI runs
  have to supply the engine's numbers.
  - **Proof at small size:** the same 15 mutants, run whole and as 4 merged

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Recorded and pushed: every commit object gets a random value in its header,
  and not a UUID.

  How I take "not a UUID", as a detail: 16 plain random bytes from the host's
  randomness, with no version bits and no text form. It is drawn once per
  transaction, so a retried upload sends the same bytes and still finds its own
  object. In the simulation it comes from the seeded host, so runs stay
  deterministic. The spec's rule that a commit object is named by the checksum
  of its content is unchanged.

  I build it right after the annotation change lands, since both change the same
  format file and would collide. The failing test for the fault is waiting on a
  branch and becomes the proof.

  Nothing else is waiting on you.

› Message from Explore
  1. Status: planned and recommended, never adopted, tried or rejected. No
  explicit rejection or deferral decision was found. The 2026-11-16 diary says
  "The repository has no property-based testing library: no Hegel, `rapid`, or
  `testing/quick` in any Go file or `go.mod`." `go.mod` and
  `internal/sqlite/crosscheck/go.mod` contain no hegel, rapid, gopter or quick
  dependency.
  2. Why it was favoured, not why it was dropped. The research was written by
  the assistant (`docs/research/testing-strategies.md` §1,
  `docs/research/sql-front-end.md` §4.3, `docs/research/graft.md` line 408). It
  recommends Hegel generators "so you get shrinking", and says "Native Go
  fuzzing is weak at deep SQL semantics because random bytes rarely form valid
  SQL. Use it for parsers and decoders, and use the Hegel generator plus PG
  differential for semantics." (`testing-strategies.md` line 230).
  `sql-front-end.md` line 270 proposes slice-1 generators "written as Hegel
  generators so that a failure shrinks". The assistant also flagged risks: "in
  beta", "0.N.0 releases may break the API", and "Pin an exact version. The API
  is still moving week to week." No quote says why it was not adopted.
  3. What Markus wanted, in his own words in the kickoff conversation
  (`docs/conversations/2026-11-10-kickoff.txt`, lines 19-23, user turn): "We'll
  get confidence in it by extensive testing: regular unit tests, integration
  tests towards a real S3 (Tigris), property-based testing with Hegel, and Go's
  built-in fuzz testing. Feel free to suggest other kinds of testing." The
  assistant's reply in that file ranks Hegel-generated SQL differential testing
  and Hegel stateful tests as items 1 and 3, and lists "Hegel produces random
  SQL and transactions" in the plan (line 672). Nothing else from Markus about

  5. What the project uses instead: its own seeded generators in

  `reached.go` check that "sometimes" paths were hit). Go native fuzzing is used
  as well; for example, `Fuzz` functions appear under `internal`

  other docs, checked via `grep` over `docs/` excluding conversations, record a
  switch away from Hegel. Why the seeded generators were chosen is not found.
  The `stmttest` README says only that tests draw "from the one `Rand`".

⏺ Hegel was never adopted, and nobody decided against it: it drifted.

  - What you asked for: in the kickoff on 2026-11-10 you listed "property-based
    testing with Hegel" among the kinds of testing.
  - What the research said: my own notes recommended Hegel generators for SQL
    "so that a failure shrinks", and flagged that it is in beta with a moving
    API.
  - What happened: the first builders wrote their own seeded generators in
    stmttest, and every later check built on those. No note records a reason,
    and I never brought it to you as a deviation. That was my miss.

  What we lack without it is mainly shrinking: a failing seed today reproduces
  the failure but does not reduce it to a small case.

  One decision for you: shall I have a builder run a bounded trial, porting one
  generated check (the engine's differential test) to Hegel and measuring
  whether it finds or shrinks anything the seeded version does not, and what it
  costs? I recommend the trial over a wholesale switch, since the seeded
  generators also feed the simulation and the planted bugs.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  Branch `text-engine`, last commit `8809cc40` (main merged in, which needed
  `upsertRefusal` called where `textRefusal` used to be). Nothing pushed.

  1. **What runs now.** `||`, `lower` and `hex` run in `set`, on either side of
  a `where` comparison, in subqueries, in `ExecAll`, and through `Prepare`,
  `Exec` and `Query`. `textRefusal` and `stmttest.TextRefusal` are gone.
  goqite's `'m_' || lower(hex(?))` with a 16-byte blob matches SQLite in `set`
  and `where`, prepared, in a transaction and after a restart (`TestText` in
  `conn_test.go`).
  2. **Refusals.** Text, also when an argument is NULL, meets only TEXT, NULL or
  other text. `lower(s) = 1` gives "an integer literal compared with an
  expression of type text"; SQLite says true for every non-null row. Arithmetic
  whose every value is NULL now has a class of its own (`classNumber`), so text
  and arithmetic never meet. Number or blob operands inside text keep the
  evaluator's refusals, at their offsets in the whole text.
  3. **Order of errors.** A `resolver` copies SQLite's walk: a missing column
  inside a call stays pending, a later missing column replaces it, and any other
  node ends the walk with it. Subqueries follow SQLite too: an update or delete
  looks up only the table, a select also its select list, and a select with
  LIMIT neither. `TestEngine_text` compares 37 such statements with SQLite. None
  is refused.
  4. **Too long.** `Bind` now gets the database's limit, and `expr.ErrTooLong`
  comes out as SQLite's own `string or blob too big` (code 18, an
  `*s4ql.Error`). I added no exported name. It comes where SQLite computes the
  text:
     - a concatenation with no column and no call, inside an expression or a SET
  value, fails before any row, also in a subquery that never runs;
     - a SET value fails per row;
     - a WHERE constant fails before any row unless it has a call and cannot
  serve the access; then it fails on the first row that reaches it;
     - a write that fails this way before any row sets the count of changes to
  0, as SQLite does.
  5. **Access.** A text constant serves key lookups and index bounds, which the
  event hooks show (`id = 'm_' || ?` reads 1 row by the key). An expression with
  a column serves nothing.
  6. **Shared rules.** `ArithmeticRefusals` types text and demands numbers only
  beside arithmetic. `Quirk` gives text no quirk. `MissingColumn` and a new
  `stmttest.MissingName` walk names as SQLite does. The drivercheck rule for
  quoted names in SET uses it, and its hand sequence includes `lower(w3) = 'a'`
  prepared, then run. I removed 7 entries from `equivalent.txt` that no longer
  hold, and updated one.
  7. **Faults found in existing code.** None. `k + 1 = s` looked like one, but
  the pass never reaches that path.
  8. **Long checks.** All passed once:
     - `TestDifferential -sequences 400`: 807 s. It ran before the review fixes
  and the merge, and I did not repeat it.
     - The whole engine package without `-short`: 436 s.
     - drivercheck, inside `make test`: 57 s.
     - `make test`, `make lint`, and the new tests at `-count=20 -race`.
  9. **Decision for you: partial statements in a transaction.** When `update t
  set s = s || s` fails partway in a write transaction, SQLite keeps the rows it
  changed before the failing row, unless it opened a statement journal (it does
  for a NOT NULL column). s4ql takes the whole statement back, as with
  arithmetic. Later reads in that transaction then differ from SQLite. Both
  sides are pinned in `TestEngine_text`.
  10. **Decision for you: cases where s4ql answers and SQLite fails.** The
  reviewers found three, open in `docs/todo/text-functions.md`. In each, s4ql
  gives no rows where SQLite gives `string or blob too big`:
      - a condition on a NULL value makes the access read nothing, so an earlier
  text condition never runs (`s || ? = 'x' and u = ?`);
      - SQLite rewrites `u = 'q' and u || ? = 'x'` so the second term is a
  constant it computes before any row;
      - a subquery constant that could serve the subquery's access is skipped,
  though SQLite's access does not use it.

      Fixing any of these means copying more of SQLite's planner.
  11. **Stricter cases, pinned and recorded.** s4ql errors where SQLite does
  not:
      - `k = 99 and s = lower(? || ?)` with `s` indexed;
      - a subquery computed where no row reaches it;
      - LIMIT 0 with a constant that serves the access;
      - a long literal inside a call.
  12. **Still open.** The generators, planted bugs and driver generated test for
  text, and mutation testing of the new code. Diary:
  `docs/diary/2026-11-26-text-engine.md`, three steps. A writer sub-agent
  drafted the new `## Text` section of `internal/engine/README.md`; I reviewed
  and corrected it.

⏺ Agent "Engine and driver for text expressions" finished · 1h 19m 59s

⏺ The engine and driver for text (||, lower, hex) are built on a branch and now
  with an independent reviewer. goqite's 'm_' || lower(hex(?)) matches SQLite,
  also prepared, in a transaction, and after a restart.

  One decision for you, because it touches the rule "SQLite's answer or an
  error".

  The case: when a text would grow past SQLite's length limit, SQLite fails with
  string or blob too big, and so does s4ql. But in three corner cases SQLite
  computes such an over-long text although no row needs it, and fails, while
  s4ql skips the computation and returns no rows. Example: where s || ? = 'x' 
  and u = ? with NULL for u: s4ql sees that nothing can match and reads nothing;
  SQLite still builds the text and fails.

  So s4ql answers where SQLite gives an error. The answer is the logically right
  one, and it only arises with a text over the limit.

  The options:

  - (a) Accept and record it. s4ql may answer where SQLite fails only because of
    the order in which its planner computes things. The differential checks get
    a narrow excuse for exactly this.
  - (b) Copy more of SQLite's planner, so that s4ql fails in the same places.
    More code whose only purpose is to reproduce an error for a statement that
    has a correct answer.

  I recommend (a). I've also asked the reviewer whether a cheap rule exists that
  makes s4ql stricter in all three cases, which would avoid the question; I'll
  tell you if it finds one.

  Which one?

  PR #182 is merged (merge commit b70138a2883da7f7cd0f00f624820d4315ebd84f,
  state MERGED). It had 30 checks and all 30 passed.

  **The five fixes:**
  1. **Close after a stop.** `sqlite.Conn.NoCheckpointOnClose`
  (`SQLITE_DBCONFIG_NO_CKPT_ON_CLOSE`) is in both bindings; the browser calls
  `sqlite3__wasm_db_config_ip`. It has tests in both, and `go test -asan` of
  `internal/sqlite` passes in Docker. Every close path checks
  `DB.keepLogLocked`. `TestDB_Close_afterLocalFailure` arms faults on every
  write, sync and truncate before the close: none fails, both files stay
  byte-identical, the next start serves every commit, and a clean close still
  checkpoints. With the fix off it fails "Expected 1, but got 2". `leftOpen` and
  the root `newWorld` now open, read and close their own connection before
  judging.
  2. **The comment in `cache.go`**, the core README and the notes now say the
  log fails its own commit and the file fails at a later commit's checkpoint.
  3. **Busy test.** `TestDB_checkpoint_busy` is in `checkpoint_cgo_test.go`: a
  third connection's full checkpoint holds the checkpoint lock, shown by a sleep
  counted in its VFS. With the planted fault it fails "Not true". It is
  cgo-only because in headless Chromium it hung in `os.CreateTemp`: Go's file
  calls need the JS event loop, which the busy handler blocks.
  4. **Telemetry.** The checkpoint event's outcome is now `busy` or `locked`.
  5. **Syncs off in tests.** The hook is `core.SyncOff(o, testonly.Key{})`. The
  key lives in `internal/core/internal/testonly`, which Go lets only packages
  under `internal/core` import. Its type `struct{ _ struct{} }` cannot be
  written as a literal elsewhere; I checked that `struct{}{}` no longer
  compiles. Other packages' tests call `internal/core/coretest`, and a new
  depguard rule `coretest` in `.golangci.yml` denies it outside test files
  (checked with a planted import). The core test helper defaults to off,
  `SyncOn` keeps the sync tests on, and `TestOpen` (core) and `TestOpen_sync`
  (root) show the production setting. The engine opens no core in production, so
  it has no such test.

  **The review of my fixes found two more holes; both are fixed and tested.**
  - A local failure that came after another stop, such as a commit that goes on
  after `Close`, still checkpointed at the close. A sticky `DB.failedLocally`
  now covers it, including a failed `Open`.
  - The first key type was `struct{}`, which `struct{}{}` forged.

⏺ The cache checkpoints change is merged (#182, all 30 checks passed).

  - What it does: the core runs the cache's checkpoints itself, and a failed
    sync or checkpoint stops the instance.
  - After a stop: closing no longer checkpoints, so a cache that may be damaged
    is not written over its own log. The review of the fix found two more holes
    of this kind; both are fixed and tested.
  - Tests: they run with syncs off unless they test syncing, guarded so that
    production code cannot turn syncs off. The saving is small: the core's CI
    job went from 5m15s to 4m28s.

    ignoring a cache error by 191 of 1,000.

  simulation: a second instance sharing the cache can still checkpoint after the
  first one stopped. The remedy conflicts with the spec's words that a cache is
  not rebuilt.

  I found no fault in `text-engine` (8809cc40). No statement gave an answer,
  type, count of changes, row of `returning` or order of errors that differs
  from SQLite without an error, and nothing panicked. I left the branch as it
  was: the probe files and `/Volumes/990/s4ql/text-engine-review/` are deleted,
  the worktree is clean, nothing was committed or pushed.

  **Statements against SQLite.** I ran about 120 new statements through the
  engine's duo (fresh state per statement, tables compared after each write) and
  a driver probe built on the drivercheck pair. All agreed with SQLite, apart
  from the recorded `s = ? || ? limit 0` stricter case and refusals the rules
  allow (invalid UTF-8 arguments, numbers in text, `?NNN`, text in IS and in the
  select list). They covered:
  - NULL in every position, empty text, `hex` of empty and NULL blobs and of
  non-ASCII text, NUL bytes inside text.
  - Text in the key and in an indexed column, without rowid. That includes
  Halloween-style updates of the column being scanned (`update q set id = id ||
  'x' where id > ''`, `set s = s || 'z' where s > 'A'`), key collisions from
  `lower(id)` and `id || 'x'`, and range bounds made from text expressions.
  - Subqueries on either side with NULL and text results, text beside arithmetic
  in one statement, and `returning`.
  - Prepared statements run several times with text, NULL, empty and blob
  arguments, text in a transaction that rolls back, and several statements in
  one Exec where the second is text or is refused.
  - Length-limit edges at 997/998 bytes under a limit of 1,000, and `hex` at
  500/501 bytes.

  **Planted faults: 32, all but three caught.**
  - 26 in the engine (operator swap, NULL rule, wrong comparison class, hoisting
  and `mayServe` variants, wrong count of changes, resolver and LIMIT stage,
  readOnly hoist) and 2 in the shared refusal rules: caught.
  - `walkNames` in `stmttest/rule.go` treating a missing column inside a call as
  the end of the walk: no test in `stmttest` catches it, because
  `TestMissingColumn_arithmetic` has no case with a call. Only drivercheck's
  `TestSequences` (the "text in expressions" sequence), in the other module,
  catches it. One case such as `lower(nosuch) = nosuch2` → `nosuch2` would close
  this. Minor.
  - Dropping `|| c.failed != nil` from `condition.perRow` survived. Probably
  equivalent: a failed condition never serves the access, because `mayServe` was
  false, and `holds` returns its error first. Read from the code, not proven.
  - `c.lazy = bound, call` in place of `text && call` survived. Equivalent,
  since only text has calls.

  **The three open cases.** I see no cheap rule that covers all three exactly.
  Two of them are cases where a column's bytes push an expression over the limit
  (`s || ?` with exactly 1,000 bytes; `u || ?` after SQLite substitutes `'q'`),
  and no check before any row can see that.
  - **Cheap partial rule:** compute every constant text subtree, with or without
  a call, before any row and in every subquery, and drop
  `lazy`/`failed`/`mayServe`. That makes s4ql give an error in case 3 and in the
  variants of cases 1 and 2 whose constant alone is too long (`lower(? || ?)`).
  It is stricter than SQLite only where the statement reads no row. It costs
  nothing in statements that work today on real data, because such a constant
  can never succeed on a row, and it removes code.
  - **Closing the rest** means copying SQLite's plan: scan the rows despite a
  NULL bound when the statement has a text expression with a column (case 1, an
  extra read), and SQLite's propagation of constants (case 2).

  What I checked and found sound: the resolver, `hoisted`/`hoist`,
  `constants`/`mayServe`, the class rules (`expressionClass`, `comparable`),
  `scalar` comparison, the SET path, `built`/`computedTooBig` for the count of
  changes, `pendingNames`, and the `stmttest` refusal rules.

  I can't see my own token count; a rough estimate is about 110k tokens.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  ## Upserts now run in the engine and the driver (branch `upsert-engine`,
  pushed)

  The engine and the driver now run `insert ... on conflict (key) do nothing`
  and `... do update set ... [where ...]`. Both differential pairs judge upserts
  by s4ql's rules, and no excuse skips them any more. I opened no pull request
  and merged nothing. The branch has 9 commits on `e671de19`. `main` has moved
  since (#181, #182, which don't touch these files), and `text-engine` had not
  landed, so I merged nothing.

  **Checks:**
  - `make test` and `make lint` pass.
  - The new tests pass with `-count=50` and under `-race` (5 to 20 counts).
  - `TestDifferential -sequences 400 -p 3` passed in 967 s.
  - `S4QL_ENGINE_SIM_SEEDS=100 TestSimulation` passed in 405 s.
  - `go test ./drivercheck/` passed in 64 s.
  - The long runs found nothing, as expected: the generators write no upserts
  yet, so these runs only show the insert path didn't regress.
  - I ran no planted bugs at 1,000, no asan, no fuzzing, and no mutation round.

  ### What is built
  - **Plan and sink.** `Insert.Conflict` holds the clause; `NewPlan` no longer
  panics, and `Format` prints the clause. The new sink `upsert`
  (`internal/engine/upsert.go`) wraps the `insert`. It inserts with `insertNew`.
  On a conflict, DO NOTHING changes nothing, and DO UPDATE reads the row with
  `get`, tests WHERE, computes SET, checks NOT NULL, and writes with the
  storage's `upsert`. `internal/core` and the evaluator are unchanged.
  - **How `excluded.c` works.** DO UPDATE reads one row of a "combined table":
  the table's columns followed by a copy of each under a name with a NUL prefix,
  which no statement text can contain. Once the names are checked with SQLite's
  messages, each `excluded.c` becomes a column of that table. The existing
  update and filter code then checks and computes it as a column of its type:
  copied alone, compared alone, and used in arithmetic.
  - **Text.** `textRefusal` now also walks SET and WHERE of DO UPDATE, so text
  stays refused there as on `main`.
  - **Tests:**
    - `TestEngine_upsert` compares every step with SQLite (answer, error offset,
  `changes()`, state), on the core and in memory, plus a replay. It covers 40
  name errors with and without a conflict, NOT NULL, read-only, and the length
  limit. It also pins s4ql's own refusals and evaluation errors, and `Format`.
    - `TestPair_upsert` runs hand-written cases through the engine's pair.
    - A driver `TestPair` subtest covers Exec, Query with RETURNING, Prepare,
  prepared runs, transactions, and several statements.
    - `TestUpsert` in `conn_test.go` goes through `database/sql`.
    - `FuzzSQL` has five upsert seeds.
  - **The pairs.**
    - `stmttest.UpsertRefusal` is replaced by `stmttest.UpsertRefusals`, an
  independent rule set over the same combined columns.
    - The engine's `pair.checkUpsert` judges evaluation errors with SQLite
  probes, each in a rolled-back savepoint. `UpsertConflictQuery` says whether
  the row conflicts. `UpsertQuirkQuery` (`do update set k = k where <guard> and
  <quirk> returning 1`) says whether the operator shows its quirk on the
  conflicting row.
    - The probes change SQLite's `changes()`, so the pair sets it back with an
  insert into a temporary WITHOUT ROWID table.
    - The driver's pair has the same rules in `checkRefused` and
  `justifyUpsert`.
  - **Docs.** New section "The engine and the driver" in `docs/todo/upserts.md`,
  the upserts line in `docs/roadmap.md`, a line in the README "Status" with its
  date stamp, and a section "Upserts" in `internal/engine/README.md` (by a
  writer sub-agent, which I reviewed). The diary
  `docs/diary/2026-11-26-upsert-engine.md` has two steps.

  ### Decisions of detail (all recorded in `docs/todo/upserts.md`)
  - Errors of names, in SQLite's measured order and at its offsets:
    - Table, then the column list and the number of values.
    - Every target column (`no such column: z` at the name), and only then `ON
  CONFLICT clause does not match any PRIMARY KEY or UNIQUE constraint`, with no
  offset.
    - SET in text order, each value before its column.
    - WHERE as for a select: an IS NULL test of a missing column, or of a
  missing `excluded.c`, gives its error at the next comparison or at the end.
    - Then RETURNING.
  - A missing `excluded.c` gives `no such column: <excluded as written>.<column
  value>`, at the offset of `excluded`.
  - Lengths are checked for the row, then SET, then WHERE, also when there is no
  conflict.
  - s4ql's refusals come after those, whatever the database holds:
    - When the statement is built: DO UPDATE's arithmetic operands, then the
  classes of SET, then WHERE.
    - Then the expressions without a column are computed (SET, then WHERE).
    - At the check, the proposed row as for an insert: NOT NULL first, then
  types.
  - Messages name an excluded column `the column excluded.c of type T`;
  `checkType` now goes through `columnName`.
  - At run time, `insertNew` measures the record first, so `string or blob too
  big` comes even with DO NOTHING on a conflict (as in SQLite). NOT NULL of the
  columns that SET gives is checked in table order, also for constants.
  - In a read-only transaction an upsert gives only name and length errors, then
  `attempt to write a readonly database`, even when it would change nothing.
  - The planted bug `checkOutside` skips upserts.

  ### Where s4ql is stricter than SQLite
  - Type refusals of DO UPDATE, and evaluation errors of its expressions without
  a column, come even when the table has no row of the key. SQLite would just
  insert there and never look at DO UPDATE. This is the same rule s4ql applies
  to an update that finds no row.
  - In a read-only transaction an upsert gives no type refusals at all, while an
  update gives those of its WHERE.

  ### Self-review (code-review skill, high)
  - **Important, fixed:** an evaluation error from a constant can come for a
  proposed row that SQLite refuses (NOT NULL or type). The conflict probe then
  failed in SQLite and the pair aborted the test instead of judging the call.
  Now a failing conflict probe counts as "no conflict", the probe runs only when
  a site has a column, and both pairs have cases for it.
  - **Nit, fixed:** in the driver's pair, Prepare refuses text before it sees an
  argument, but Exec refuses an unsupported argument first. Cases added for
  both.
  - **Nit, fixed:** the README Status date stamp, and the diary steps.
  - **Nit, left open:** an upsert's event has no `statement.access` and no
  `statement.rows_read`, just like an insert, although DO UPDATE reads the row
  with `get`. Recorded as open in `docs/todo/upserts.md`.

  ### What is open (each a later builder's)
  - The generators write no upserts: workload with differential, planted bugs in
  the simulation (none touches `upsert.go`), and the driver's generated test.
  - For a call where the engine gives no error, the pair's due check covers
  every DO UPDATE operator, but only with these hand-written cases until the
  generators exist.
  - Mutation testing of `upsert.go` and `stmttest/upsert.go`.
  - Several rows per insert, and the forms the statement pass refuses.

  ### Notes
  - A stray empty file `off` (from a `sqlite3` call in the worktree) was
  committed by mistake and then removed in a later commit.
  - Scratch files are in `/Volumes/990/s4ql/upsert-engine/` (logs and `tmp`); I
  will delete that directory when told the work is merged.
  - No design or scope question came up.
  - Token use: roughly 330,000 in my context, plus about 146,000 for the writer
  and 249,000 for the review agents.

  - /Users/maragubot/Developer/s4ql/.claude/worktrees/agent-a7c1a16364d648265/in

  I found no wrong answer without an error on branch `upsert-engine` at
  89e148f3. Two planted engine faults went uncaught by every test, and three of
  five oracle faults went uncaught.

  **Faults in s4ql:** none found. I compared about 60 hand-written upserts
  against SQLite 3.54.1, on the core and in memory. Each step compared the rows,
  the error, `changes()` and the table's state. Every case matched SQLite or
  was refused as the notes say. Since everything matched, the cases are listed
  in the last paragraph.

  **Engine faults that no short test caught** (I ran the upsert tests of engine,
  stmttest, the root package and the driver's `TestPair` subtest; the second
  fault was reasoned, not checked):
  1. **NOT NULL checked in the order of the text instead of the table**
  (`upsert.run`, `for i := range u.set.positions` in place of `u.set.order`).
  With `create table u (k int primary key, a int not null, b int not null)
  strict, without rowid` and row (1,1,1), `insert into u values (1,2,2) on
  conflict (k) do update set b = null, a = null` gives `NOT NULL constraint
  failed: u.a` in both SQLite and s4ql. That is correct today, but nothing pins
  it: `upsertSetUp` has only one NOT NULL column besides the key. A table with
  two NOT NULL columns should be added to `TestEngine_upsert`.
  2. **The length check of arguments in SET arithmetic removed**
  (`argumentLengths` in `build.doUpdate`). The notes claim the order "lengths of
  SET" before the type refusals, but no case has a too-long argument inside SET
  arithmetic, such as `set v = v + ?` with a long blob under a limit. A refusal
  would probably have come instead of `string or blob too big`.

  **Oracle faults:**
  - Caught (2): reading a failed or inverted conflict probe the wrong way round,
  and the rules giving no refusals for WHERE.
  - Not caught (3), all in `pair.checkUpsert`:
    - accepting any "not supported" message;
    - ignoring a refusal that was due but not given;
    - treating every evaluation error as justified.

    The pair has no negative self-test for upserts. Engine faults are caught
  only by the cases pinned in `TestEngine_upsert`, the root `TestUpsert` and the
  driver test. This fits the planted bugs being a later step, but until then a
  wrong verdict of the pair goes unnoticed.

  **Engine faults that were caught:**
  - DO NOTHING counting 1, and DO UPDATE counting 0;
  - a write when WHERE is false;
  - `excluded.c` reading the stored row, and the stored row reading
  `excluded.c`;
  - NOT NULL of SET skipped;
  - RETURNING giving the row before the change;
  - a target that is not the key accepted;
  - the errors of names: a missing name in a NULL test, the names of SET
  skipped, a SET column checked before its value;
  - the type checks of SET and of WHERE skipped, and type refusals in a
  read-only transaction;
  - the offset of `excluded`, the constants of WHERE, the `excluded.c` name in
  messages;
  - the length of a SET literal, and the text refusal of DO UPDATE.

  **One thing seen, not verified:** DO UPDATE `... where 1 = 1` and `where ? =
  1` (with NULL) are refused, where SQLite runs them. I take this to be the
  update's existing rule for comparisons without a column, but did not check
  that an update refuses them too.

  **Checked and sound:**
  - The NUL-prefixed combined columns can't be reached: `stmt.Check` refuses
  every NUL byte (`not supported: NUL byte`) in CREATE TABLE names, selects and
  `excluded."\x00v"`. Names are checked against the real table before the
  combined one is used, and RETURNING is built on the real table.
  - The cases I compared against SQLite, all matching:
    - targets in another case or in double quotes; DEFAULT and NULL read through
  `excluded.c`; NOT NULL of the proposed row and of SET, also with DO NOTHING;
    - WHERE that is NULL or compares `excluded` with the key; RETURNING after
  each outcome;
    - indexed columns changed by DO UPDATE and read back through the index;
    - arguments of every type in VALUES, SET and WHERE;
    - insert, update, delete, update and upsert again on one key in a
  transaction, then rollback, and a failing NOT NULL inside a committed
  transaction;
    - an upsert first, middle and last of several statements in `ExecAll`,
  including failures partway: the state and the count matched, apart from the
  count after s4ql's own overflow error, which is an excused case.
  - The probes in rolled-back savepoints and the reset of `changes()` through
  the temporary table look right, and stay harmless in a read-only transaction.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  The fault is fixed and merged in pull request #183
  (https://github.com/maragudk/s4ql/pull/183). It had 29 checks, all in `pass`,
  and `gh pr view` says MERGED. Branch: `blob-argument`.

  - **Where the bytes were kept, and the fix:** `argument` in `/conn.go` stored
  the caller's `[]byte` as the parameter's value. The engine reads that value
  only when rows are read: a select's condition and a RETURNING's rows both read
  it at `Next`. The fix is one `bytes.Clone(v)` in `argument`, which every path
  calls once per argument: `Exec`, `Query`, prepared, in a transaction and
  outside one, and each statement of several in one `Exec`. A nil `[]byte` is
  still refused, and the empty blob stays a blob.
  - **The review's test:** it is now the first two subtests of `TestArgument` in
  `/conn_test.go`. They failed at `main` with `Expected "1", but got "2"` and
  `Expected "10", but got "20"`. I widened the select case to four kinds of byte
  argument (`[]byte`, a named slice type, `sql.RawBytes`, a `driver.Valuer`) on
  four paths.
  - **1. Write changed after `Exec`, before `Commit`: sound.** The third subtest
  passed at `main`, before the fix. It overwrites the buffers in one
  transaction after a plain `Exec`, a prepared `Exec`, a two-statement `Exec`
  and a RETURNING. The cache, the commit object's annotations and a second
  instance that replays the log all hold the original bytes. The reason is that
  the core writes changes into SQLite, which copies them at bind, and serializes
  each annotation to its spool during `Tx.Annotate`. Only the RETURNING row
  given back in the transaction was wrong, and the fix covers it.
  - **2. Rows handed to the caller: sound.** `TestRows_blob` in `/rows_test.go`
  passed at `main`. `value` in `/rows.go` copies every blob, and `ColumnBlob` in
  `internal/sqlite` uses `C.GoBytes`. A `[]byte` from `Scan` stays the same
  after the next row, the close and later updates. Changing a `sql.RawBytes` or
  a scanned `any` changes nothing stored. That is stronger than what
  `database/sql` promises for `RawBytes`.
  - **3. Values kept across calls: sound.** The core keeps a transaction's
  writes in SQLite temporary tables, not in memory. The engine's `Conn` and
  `transaction` keep no values beyond the rows of the last query, which now hold
  copies. No event logs a value. Nothing in `internal/core` needs another
  builder.
  - **Checks:** `make test`, `make lint`, and the new tests at `-count=50`, also
  under `-race`, passed. `/code-review low` found nothing. `main` was merged in
  before the push and the short tests passed again.
  - **Notes:** `docs/todo/driver.md` had no open item for this, so the record is
  the diary at `/docs/diary/2026-11-26-blob-argument.md`. The scratch directory
  `/Volumes/990/s4ql/blob-argument` is deleted, and no watch is left running.
  - **Tokens:** about 120,000, plus 50,000 for the reviewer.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)
⏺ Agent "Fix driver keeping caller's bytes" finished · 30m 54s

⏺ The fault from the adversarial review is fixed and merged (#183, 29 checks
  passed): the driver now copies a blob argument when it receives it.

  The worse variant does not exist. A write whose bytes the caller changes
  between Exec and Commit was already safe before the fix: the cache, the commit
  object, and a second instance replaying the log all held the original bytes,
  because SQLite copies them at bind. Only rows read back in the same call (a
  select's condition, a returning) were wrong.

  The builder also checked the other directions and found them sound: rows
  handed to the caller are copies, and nothing in the engine or the core keeps a
  caller's bytes across calls.

  The Hegel trial is done and pushed on `hegel-trial` (6 commits, no PR). Hegel
  adds one thing nothing else here gives: shrinking a failing series of
  statements to a few plain lines. It found no fault the seeded checks miss. I
  recommend adopting it pinned, for series checked against SQLite and for pure
  components with an oracle.

  **What it needs:** `hegel.dev/go/hegel v0.10.3` (2026-11-24). It needs Go
  1.26.1 and adds `purego` at an alpha pseudo-version plus `x/sys`. The Rust
  engine `libhegel` is embedded in the module (about 5 MB per platform) and
  loaded without cgo, so nothing gets installed; it ran here on the first `go
  test`. It does not build for js/wasm (`purego: undefined: syscallArgs`), so a
  Hegel test in a browser package would need `//go:build !(js && wasm)`. It is
  beta: 0.10.0 changed the API, and 0.10.1–0.10.3 came within five days. CI
  determinism comes from `WithDerandomize(true)`, `WithDatabase("")` and
  suppressing `TooSlow`; it has no other timing dependence, and `-short` gives
  10 cases.

  **Tests (both pass at main, also under -race):**
  - `TestHegel_cases` (`internal/expr`): typed trees checked through the
  existing `checker.check`. 5,000 cases, about 2 s.
  - `TestHegel_series` (`internal/engine`): up to 30 statements through the
  existing `pair.step`, with restarts, the length limit and scan directions
  drawn. 200 series, about 7 s. Each series gets its own `testing.TB`
  (`seriesT`), so a helper's `Fatal` becomes a failure Hegel can shrink and
  every series closes its own instances.

  | Faults | Seeded check | Hegel as committed |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  | 10 planted by hand in `expr.go` | 10/10, within 164 cases, 2–27 nodes |
  7/10, shrunk to 2–10 nodes |
  | 11 planted bugs of the engine | 11/11 in the 1st–5th sequence, failing at
  statement 16–38 of 48, in odd spellings | 10/11 within 139 series, shrunk to
  2–5 statements (8 to 2–3) |

  - The faults Hegel misses need related values: `MIN/-1`, a product at the

  `limitFirst`, which needs many rows.

  returning *`.

  - `FuzzExpr` failed straight away on a seed-corpus entry, the generator's own
  unminimized 27-node case.
  - Driving the existing expr generator through a Hegel-backed `rand.Source`

› Message from fabrik:builder
  All four points are done and I found no fault in `upsert.go`; the branch
  `upsert-engine-fixes` is pushed (5 commits on top of `89e148f3`), no PR
  opened, no planted fault committed.
  1. NOT NULL order: `TestEngine_upsert` now has a table `u (k, a not null, b
  not null)`. `set b = null, a = null` gives SQLite's `NOT NULL constraint
  failed: u.a`. With `upsert.run` checking in the order of SET
  (`u.set.positions`), the test gets `u.b` and fails.
  2. Argument length: the reviewer was right. With `argumentLengths` disabled in
  `build.doUpdate`, the whole engine package passed (`ok ... 383.918s`). New
  cases on a table `w` under a limit of 1,000 put a long blob or text argument
  in arithmetic, in SET (`v = v + ?` with and without a conflict, `r =
  excluded.r * ?`) and in WHERE. SQLite gives `string or blob too big` for each.
  The fault now fails the test ("got not supported"). WHERE goes through
  `build.values` (shared with update), and disabling either of its two branches
  fails a case too (`where v + ? = 1`, `where v = excluded.v + ?`).
  3. Pairs: a new subtest of `TestPair_upsert` feeds `pair.checkUpsert` wrong
  answers next to right ones, and all three faults now fail it. These are: any
  "not supported" accepted, a due refusal not given, and every evaluation error
  treated as justified (checked for another value, without a conflict, for a
  constant, and read-only).
     - In the driver, the old upsert subtest passed under faults 1 and 3 (`want
  = r.err` in `checkRefused`, `return ""` in `justifyUpsert`).

› Message from fabrik:builder
  #186 is merged (merge commit 34c3043a224eaa4c8ba0c245cb95c48488e586ff).
  Upserts now run on main, and `||`, `lower` and `hex` work in SET and WHERE of
  DO UPDATE as they do in an update. CI had 29 checks, all pass or skipping. The
  worktree and branch `upsert-engine-land` are left for you; my scratch
  directory is deleted.

  1. **The merge.** Four conflicts. `textRefusal` and `stmttest.TextRefusal`
  went, as on main; both new README sections stay. Main still called
  `upsertRefusal` (in `Check`, `prepare`, `execPart`), which the branch had
  deleted, so those calls went. `doUpdate` needed main's new signatures. The
  pairs' calls of `TextRefusal` went. A second merge brought in the Hegel tests
  (#185); `TestHegel_series` passes without changes.
  2. **How text in DO UPDATE behaves** (measured with the 3.54.1 shell, and
  compared with SQLite in the tests):
     - **Types:** as in an update. Text meets only TEXT, NULL or text; a number
  or blob inside text gets the evaluator's refusal at its offset. SET's refusals
  come before WHERE's, also without a conflict.
     - **Names:** SQLite's walk through `resolver`, which now treats
  `excluded.c` as a column. `whereNames` copies `build.walk`. So `set s =
  lower(excluded.zz) || yy` gives `no such column: yy`.
     - **Too long, also without a conflict:** SQLite runs DO UPDATE's constants
  at the start of the insert. So the hoisted subtrees and WHERE sides with no
  column and no call fail with `string or blob too big` even when there is no
  conflict.
     - **Too long, only on the conflict row:** a SET value with no column, a
  WHERE side with a call, and text with a column. DO UPDATE's WHERE never serves
  an access, so `doUpdate` gives `b.constants` a nil table and `mayServe`
  returns false.
     - **Refused forms:** none.
  3. **Where s4ql differs:** stricter on types (SQLite orders or converts). A
  literal longer than the limit inside a call fails even without a conflict;
  SQLite computes it only on the conflict row. That is the same as main's
  update, and a test pins it.
  4. **Change to main's update rule in the engine's pair:** `pair.checkLength`
  used to reject that last difference, for updates on main as well as for
  upserts. It now accepts `string or blob too big` there, so it judges updates
  differently too.
  5. **Planted faults:** nine, never committed, and each failed a short
  `TestEngine_upsert` subtest. The pair missed three of them, which exposed two
  gaps: its subtest under the limit deleted the test key too rarely, and it had
  no names-inside-a-call case. Both are fixed and those three now fail the pair
  too.
  6. **Review:** two independent reviewers found no divergence from SQLite in
  about 790 upserts. They found test gaps, stale comments, and the `checkLength`
  gap above, all fixed.
  7. **Long runs**, all passed: `TestDifferential` at 400 sequences (738 s),
  `TestSimulation` at 100 seeds (404 s), and `./drivercheck/` (89 s). `make
  test` and `make lint` pass, and the new tests pass at `-count=50` and `-race
  -count=20`.
  8. **Open:**
     - Both reviewers suggested sharing `whereNames` with `build.walk`, and DO
  UPDATE's SET constants with `build.change`. I didn't, because it touches the
  update's code.
     - The driver's pair can't judge a prepared statement whose runs mix
  refusals and successes. This is not new: it fails the same way for an update.
     - Generated checks and planted simulation bugs for upserts, and mutation
  testing, are still later builders' work.

※ recap: Goal: run glue on s4ql; today text functions, upserts, and Hegel tests landed, and six agents are still running checks and fixes. Next action for you: decide the over-long text question, accept and record (a) or copy SQLite's planner (b).

⏺ Agent "Adversarial review of remaining parts" is waiting on its own background
  work · 1h 13m 5s

› Message from fabrik:builder
  Branch `update-order` is pushed (12 commits on origin/main). There's no pull
  request, as the brief asked. The engine now gives SQLite's error for the
  review's two cases and for every rule I measured. It still differs from SQLite
  where SQLite's planner chooses another index than the engine's rules, and the
  generated workload never reaches the case.

  1. **SQLite's rules (measured with the 3.54.1 shell and EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN,
  then checked in update.c and where.c):**
     - An update runs in one pass and changes the rows in its access's order.
  That is key order for a point, a key range or a scan. For an index equality or
  range it is index order, then the key, so a desc column runs backward. SQLite
  always reads forward.
     - It falls back to two passes in key order when the update sets a key
  column (even `k = k`), sets a column of the index it reads by (another index's
  column doesn't count), has a subquery in WHERE (one in SET doesn't), or has
  RETURNING.
     - In one pass, a later row's WHERE error comes after an earlier row's SET
  error.
     - RETURNING rows are always in key order. A delete can't fail per row in
  what s4ql supports, and `changes()` is 0 after any row failure, so neither
  depends on the order.
  2. **The engine now:** `changeOrder` in `internal/engine/operator.go` follows
  those rules, using the access the rules choose even under a forced test path.
  The new `core.Tx.KeysBy` in `internal/core/tx.go` gives the keys in index
  order: it numbers them with `row_number()` into a second temp table. The
  deferred WHERE error is in `changeRows`. `TestEngine_updateOrder` checks every
  rule against SQLite on both instances, and `TestTx_KeysBy` checks the core.
  3. **Not matched:** SQLite picks its access by estimated cost, and the rules
  differ from it in five measured cases:
     - an update with no WHERE, which SQLite reads through the narrowest index
  that holds the WHERE's columns
     - a tie between indexes, which SQLite gives to the last created and the
  rules to the first
     - two bounds beating one
     - `is null` against `=`
     - `is not null` used as a range

     Smallest case: table `t (k int primary key, a int, x int not null, y int
  not null, nx int, ny int)` with index `ia (a)`, rows `(1,20,1,1,null,1)` and
  `(2,10,1,1,1,null)`. `update t set x = nx, y = ny` gives `t.y` in SQLite and
  `t.x` in s4ql. Whether an update's access rules should follow SQLite's costs
  is a design question for you or Markus.
  4. **Generated checks, before:** the workload never produced the case, and no
  excuse was hiding it. A default run had 38 NOT NULL and 6 too-big update
  failures by an index, but in none did the order decide the error: each failing
  row gets its NULL from one literal or one column, so the failing rows fail
  alike. `recordSlips` replayed rows in key order; it now uses the engine's
  order.
  5. **Generated checks, now:** `pair.checkUpdateOrder` replays SQLite's order
  row by row, using its plan, when SQLite's order isn't key order or the answers
  differ, and counts "update whose first row to fail is another in SQLite's
  order than in the order of the key". The new excuse `excusedPlannerOrder`
  (cases in item 3, and calls SQLite runs with subqueries replaced by values)
  holds only if each side's error is its own first failing row, replayed one at
  a time. `TestPair_updateOrder` covers both. The kind is not required yet
  because nothing reaches it.
  6. **Long runs, after the last change:**
     - `TestDifferential -sequences 400 -p 3`: passed in 926 s. Its first try
  failed at once on a false failure in my new pair code: SQLite's plan spells
  the table as the statement does, and the pair looked for the stored spelling.
  Fixed and pinned. No fault in s4ql showed up. I did not check whether the

     - The simulation with 100 seeds: passed in 457 s.

     - New tests pass with `-count=50` and `-race`.
     - The code-review found six issues; all are fixed and recorded in the

⏺ Agent(Independent review of update order) Opus 5.5  ⎿  Backgrounded agent (↓ to manage · ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Agent "Fix order of rows in an update" finished · 1h 19m 52s

⏺ The fix for the order of an update's rows is built and with an independent
  reviewer. It matches SQLite for every rule the builder measured. One part
  cannot be matched, and it raises the same question as the over-long text, so
  I'm putting both to you as one decision, replacing the earlier a/b.

  The question: where SQLite's result depends on the choices of its cost-based
  planner, does s4ql copy the planner?

  Where it shows up:

  - Which error an update gives. When two rows fail in different ways, the error
    is that of the row changed first, and SQLite picks its access by estimated
    cost. In five measured cases it picks another index than s4ql's rules do
    (for example a tie between two indexes goes to the last created). Both sides
    fail and change nothing; only the message differs.
  - The over-long text. In three corner cases SQLite computes a text past the
    length limit that no row needs, and fails; s4ql returns no rows.

  The options:

  - (a) No. s4ql keeps its own rules for choosing an access. A difference that
    arises only from SQLite's planner making another choice is a recorded
    exception to "SQLite's answer or an error", each one named and checked by
    the differential tests through a narrow excuse.
  - (b) Yes. s4ql copies SQLite's cost estimates and rewrites, so the same
    statement fails the same way. That ties s4ql's planner to SQLite's internals
    permanently.

  I recommend (a). The first kind is harmless. The second is the one to weigh:
  s4ql gives a correct answer where SQLite gives an error, but only for a text
  past the length limit.

  If (a), the spec needs a sentence for it, and I'd propose the wording next.
