  I found no case where s4ql and SQLite give different results on this branch,
  but I found two gaps in the tests. Worktree is back at origin/update-order
  with no changes, and the scratch directory is deleted.

  1. **Wrong tie order goes unnoticed (most severe).** I made the engine break
  ties among equal index values by the primary key in reverse, in
  `build.changeOrder` (`internal/engine/operator.go`). No engine test catches
  this: not the targeted ones, not a default `TestDifferential`, not
  `TestEngine_*`. Only `TestTx_KeysBy` in core would see a matching fault in
  core. A two-row statement exposes it:
     - Setup: `t (k int primary key, b int, x int not null, y int not null, nx
  int, ny int)`, `ib (b)`, rows `(1,5,1,1,null,1)` and `(2,5,1,1,1,null)`.
     - Statement: `update t set x = nx, y = ny where b = 5`.
     - SQLite gives `t.x`; the faulty engine gives `t.y`.
     - The fix is to add this case to `TestEngine_updateOrder`. Its equality
  case uses `iab (b, a)`, so ties there are settled by `a`, not by the key.
  2. **The excuse can be widened without any test noticing.** I changed the last
  line of `pair.checkUpdateOrder` from `got.err == byEngine` to
  `rowFailure(byEngine)`. That accepts any row error from the engine in a
  planner case. It passes `TestPair_*`, `TestDifferential` and
  `TestDifferential_plantedBugs`. No case in `TestPair_updateOrder` has a wrong
  engine error where SQLite's order does give SQLite's error. The fix is a
  `checkUpdateOrder` call with SQLite's error in that order and a third error
  from the engine, which must be refused.

  **My checks of the rules against SQLite.** They match for:
  - ranges and equalities on ascending and descending indexes, and on a
  two-column index mixing `asc` and `desc`;
  - NULLs in an index column, ascending and descending, and ties settled by the
  key;
  - an equality with a range on the key, and `between`;
  - the fall-backs: setting the second column of the index, setting a column of
  another index, a subquery in WHERE, RETURNING;
  - WHERE errors: one in a later row is deferred behind an earlier row's SET
  error, also on a descending index, and one in the first row comes at once.

  A subquery in SET, `in (...)`, `or`, `is not`, multi-row inserts and a primary
  key of several columns are refused as not supported, so they can't diverge.
  The only differences I saw are the documented path dependence of a forced
  scan-all path, and the overflow evaluation error that s4ql gives by its own
  rule. Successful results can't change, since without unique indexes and with
  key changes falling back, success doesn't depend on order.

  **What `KeysBy` costs.** I timed `update t set x = x + 1 where a > 0` over
  10,000 rows with index `ia`, 5 iterations per run:
  - Merge base: about 163 ms per update (3 runs).
  - Branch: about 212 ms (5 runs), so roughly 30% slower.
  - A one-row equality showed no difference (0.5–1.2 ms on both).

  Every one-pass update by an index pays this, even when no row fails. One idea
  for the lead: run in key order, and only on a row failure roll back to the
  statement's savepoint and replay in index order. Failing statements would then
  pay twice instead of every update paying.

  **Lifetime of the temporary tables.** Names are unique per transaction. Both
  tables are dropped by `dropKeys`, including a half-made set. A rollback to the
  statement's savepoint undoes their creation too, so I see no leftover or
  stale read. `KeysBy` reads only the values it was given, so earlier writes and
  deletes in the same transaction don't affect it.

  **Planted faults the tests did catch:** index order ignored; `desc` read
  forward in core; RETURNING fall-back dropped; index-column fall-back dropped;
  subquery fall-back dropped; WHERE error raised at once; WHERE error deferred
  in two passes as well; ties ignored in core. Dropping the index-name
  comparison in `checkUpdateOrder` also passed, but that check is redundant, so
  it isn't a finding.

  **Sound otherwise:** the rules, `changeOrder`, the deferral in `changeRows`,
  `KeysBy`'s SQL (window sort with NULLs first ascending, total order through
  the key, `unique` plus `insert or ignore`), and the documents.

  About 150k tokens.

› Message from general-purpose
  Parts 13, 15 and 7 now each have an outside review by agy, run one at a time
  in that order. agy found two faults that matter. Each has a failing test, and
  the code is not fixed. Branch `adversarial-codebase-2` is pushed. The record
  is `docs/research/adversarial-codebase-2026-11-26.md`, in the sections
  "Summary of the second round" and "The second round".

  **The faults that matter:**
  1. **`Stmt.StepContext` hides an I/O error behind an ended context**
  (`TestAdversarial_stepContextHidesIOError`,
  `internal/sqlite/adversarial_test.go`). A read of the cache fails in a step
  whose context has just ended. On its way out of the failed step SQLite calls
  the progress handler again, and it overwrites the I/O error with
  SQLITE_INTERRUPT, so `StepContext` returns `context.Canceled`. SQLite has
  already rolled back the connection's whole transaction for the I/O error. The
  core takes the error for a harmless stop and leaves the write transaction open
  (`Tx.ScanIndex`, `index.go:1155`), so the instance does not stop. The fault
  is rare: the context must end within the step's last progress period, at most
  10,000 instructions. What the core does next was traced, not run: the next
  statement's savepoint opens a transaction of its own in the cache, so a write
  could reach the cache that is not in the log.
  2. **A commit that is not committed wraps the cause of its context**
  (`TestAdversarial_commitErrorWrapsCause`, root `adversarial_test.go`). The
  cause comes in through `after(context.Cause(ctx), …)` in `tx.go:1596` and the
  `%w` in `tx.go:1449`. `errgroup.WithContext` cancels with the first
  goroutine's error, so another goroutine's "not committed" error can match
  `ErrOutcomeUnknown`, SQLite's `*Error` (a UNIQUE constraint, say) or
  `driver.ErrBadConn`. The documents promise the opposite for each of these. The
  test confirms all three at `main`.

  **Claims per part:**

  | Part | agy's claims | Confirmed | No consequence | Wrong | Other |
  | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
  | 13, SQLite binding | 4 | 1 | 2 | 1 | |

⏺ Agent "Adversarial review of remaining parts" finished · 1h 51m 12s

  Searched for 1 pattern, ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ The second round of the adversarial review (agy on the three parts codex had
  declined) found two more faults that matter. Both have failing tests, and a
  builder is on each.

  1. A step can hide an I/O error behind an ended context. This is the serious
  one. If a read of the cache fails in the same moment the caller's context
  ends, the binding reports "cancelled" instead of the I/O error. SQLite has
  already rolled back its transaction, but the core takes it for a harmless stop
  and carries on. The reviewer traced, without running it, that a later write
  could then reach the cache without being in the log. The window is tiny, but
  this is the kind of fault the project exists to rule out. The builder first
  makes that consequence concrete in a test, then fixes both the binding and the
  core; an independent review follows before it lands.

  2. A failed commit wraps the cause of the caller's context. With errgroup, one
  goroutine's "not committed" error can then match whatever another goroutine
  failed with: ErrOutcomeUnknown, a SQLite constraint error, or
  driver.ErrBadConn, which makes database/sql retry. The builder also checks
  whether s4ql's own code can be steered that way.

  and the builder found it was worse than reported.

  - The reported fault: a failed commit wrapped the cause of the caller's
    context. It now returns the plain context error and never the cause.
  - The worse form: s4ql's own code could be steered by a caller's cause. After
    a refused commit, the manifest read that followed could fail with the cause;
    if that cause happened to be another instance's stop error, the read

    it at the old main. The store's contract now forbids errors that carry the
    cause, and both stores comply.

    cut-off error answer from the cause, so a real 400, 412, or 404 could be
    misread as "too large", "changed", or "not found". Fixed with a test.

  matching them, so its safety rests on that new contract. Classifying by where

› Message from fabrik:builder
  I fixed both layers and pushed `step-context` (4 commits on `origin/main`, no
  pull request). At `main` the consequence is real: a write that is not in the
  log reaches the cache, is served, and survives a restart.
  1. **At `main`:** after the hidden rollback in a write transaction, the next
  upsert ran outside any transaction and SQLite committed its row to the cache.
  The upsert then failed (`no such table: temp.s4ql_touched_1`), the commit
  failed with "can only be rolled back", and the instance kept serving the row.
  A restart on the same cache still served it, while a new cache replaying the
  log did not have it. If the scan came first, readers saw the row before the
  commit, which was then acknowledged. In a read transaction, a later scan saw a
  commit made after the read began. I made the failure deterministic with a
  progress period of 1 (new `Conn.SetProgressPeriod`, `Options.ProgressPeriod`
  for tests) and a context that ends once faultvfs has failed the read. No
  timing is involved.
  2. **Binding (`internal/sqlite/sqlite.go`):** the progress handler stops a
  step only while `sqlite3_stmt_busy` is true, so a step that ended or failed
  keeps its own result. A stop that rolled back an explicit transaction now
  gives SQLITE_INTERRUPT, not the context's error. `Conn.Autocommit` is new in
  both builds. This also fixed a second hidden case: an insert in autocommit
  mode was reported as stopped after it had committed.
  3. **Core (`conn.harmless` in `errors.go`, used in `Tx.scan` and
  `ReadTx.scan`):** while the core's transaction is open, a failed step after
  which the connection has left the cache's transaction is the new local failure
  `errTxEnded`, which stops the instance. Each layer alone passes the
  end-to-end test, and every new test fails without its fix.
  4. **Other places checked:** `Exec`, prepare and the checkpoints take no
  context, so `StepContext` is the only place the binding turns a stop into a
  context's error. The browser build never stops a step. In the core, only the
  first step of a scan and the count of its unread rows step with a context;
  gets, writes and the commit do not.
  5. **Self-review:** two reviewers agreed on a regression in my first core
  check. A loop body that calls `Tx.Rollback` or `ReadTx.Close` and then breaks
  out of a sorted scan made a context ending during the count stop the instance.
  I fixed it and added a test. On their other points, the binding's I/O test no
  longer loops on instruction counts that a SQLite upgrade could break, and the
  autocommit checks moved into C.
  6. **Checks, all passed:** `make test`, `make lint`, `internal/core` without
  `-short`, the new tests at `-count=50` and under `-race`, `make test-asan` on
  `internal/sqlite`, and the browser run of the new core and binding tests.
  7. **Open:** the simulation cannot produce this fault. It has no read faults
  of the cache and its contexts end by timeout, so a planted bug would need item
  2 of the disk-failure list. I recorded this and the fix in
  `docs/todo/storage-core-tests.md`; the diary is
  `docs/diary/2026-11-26-step-context.md`.

⏺ Agent "Fix step hiding an I/O error" finished · 1h 5m 35s

⏺ The serious fault from the review is real, and it is fixed on a branch that is
  now with an independent reviewer.

  What the builder showed at main: when a read of the cache fails in the moment
  the caller's context ends, SQLite rolls back its transaction, the core does
  not notice, and the next upsert runs outside any transaction. SQLite commits
  that row to the cache. The instance served it, and it survived a restart,
  while a fresh cache replaying the log did not have it. So the cache held a
  write that was not in the log. The test is deterministic, with no timing
  involved.

  The fix, in both layers:

  - Binding: a step that has already failed keeps its own error; the context can
    no longer overwrite it. This also fixed a second hidden case: an insert
    reported as cancelled after it had in fact committed.
  - Core: after any failed step, if SQLite has left the transaction the core
    believes is open, the instance stops.

  Each layer alone passes the end-to-end test, and every new test fails without
  its fix.

  The review checks whether the hole is closed on every path and not only where
  the test stands, plants faults, and gets one outside opinion each from agy and
  codex, since agy found this one.

  A gap this exposes: the simulation could not have produced this fault. It has
  no read faults of the cache, and its contexts only end by timeout. That is
  item 2 on the list of disk failures to add, and it moves up in priority.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  I pushed branch `update-order-2` (head d05642ad, origin/main merged in
  cleanly). No pull request opened, as the brief said.

  1. **How the replay works.** An update runs in key order again, as on main.
  When it fails with a row error (a WHERE error, a constraint, `string or blob
  too big`, or a SET evaluation error: `failsRow`) and SQLite's order is an
  index's, `runSink` rolls back to the statement's savepoint, reopens it, and
  replays in that order with step 2's rules (`KeysBy`, the deferred WHERE
  error). The statement then fails with the replay's error (`replayer` in
  `/internal/engine/operator.go`, `runSink` and `runSavepointed` in
  `/internal/engine/engine.go`).
  2. **What it cannot do wrong.** A statement of its own takes a savepoint only
  for an update that may replay, and always releases it. In a transaction, the
  savepoint is open again after the replay. Nothing of the replay stays,
  changes() is that of a failed statement, and the event counts only the first
  run's rows read. A replay that succeeds is an internal error. The one
  exception is the planted bug `evalStops`, which stops the replay and succeeds,
  as it does a run.
  3. **Measurements.** The clock varied 30–55% under a load of 16–28 from other
  agents, so these are CPU time per statement over 10,000 rows. No failure: main
  157–168 ms, now 165–173 ms (1–5% over three rounds, within a round's spread),
  step 2 186 ms. A copy without the savepoint measured the same. A failing
  update: main 80 ms, step 2 88 ms, now 174 ms, about 2.2x, because the run and
  the replay each change about 5,000 rows. Numbers are in
  `/docs/todo/update-order.md`.
  4. **Planted faults, never committed, each failing only its test.**
     - Ties broken by the key in reverse in `build.changeOrder` fail the new `ib
  (b)` case in `TestEngine_updateOrder`.
     - `got.err == byEngine` widened to `rowFailure(byEngine)` fails
  `TestPair_updateOrder`.
     - Also checked: dropping the rollback, dropping `CodeTooBig` or `ofRow`
  from `failsRow`, and counting the replay's reads each fail one new test.
  5. **Review.** Both code-review reviewers found no wrong error. Both found
  that `evalStops` turned a replay into an internal error, which
  `TestSimulation_plantedBugs` would not have accepted; fixed. I also added the
  tests they asked for and fixed stale comments and README lines.
  6. **Checks.** `make test` and `make lint` passed. The new tests pass with
  `-count=50` and `-race -count=3`. `equivalent.txt` names were renamed for the
  split of `update.run` into `run`/`runIn`, plus one entry for `runSink`.
  7. **Long runs**, after the last code change, all passed and found nothing:
  TestDifferential at `-sequences 400` in 1,105 s, TestSimulation at 100 seeds
  in 466 s, drivercheck in 61 s.
  8. **Open.** The `evalStops` change has not been run at 1,000 seeds (brief
  said no planted-bug runs; only `TestRunSink` checks it), so the nightly is its
  first run. There is no cancel test of an update by an index. The generator
  that reaches the case and the planner question are as in step 2. Scratch
  `/Volumes/990/s4ql/update-order-2/` (bench trees, logs) stays until merge.
  Tokens: about 250k in my context, plus the reviewers' 211k and 251k.

⏺ Agent "Independent review of step-context fix" is waiting on its own
  background work · 33m 21s

› Message from general-purpose
  Review of `step-context` (c0d3a2aa): I found no path where the invariant
  fails. After every failed call on a cache connection, either the core's view
  of the transaction still matches `sqlite3_get_autocommit`, or the instance
  stops before it runs another statement on that connection.

  1. **Which errors roll back on their own.** I read SQLite 3.54.1's source
  (`internal/sqlite/c/sqlite3.c`, `sqlite3VdbeHalt`). Only four errors make
  SQLite roll back the whole transaction by itself: NOMEM, IOERR, FULL, and
  INTERRUPT. INTERRUPT does so only for a statement that writes; NOMEM and FULL
  roll back just the statement when it uses a statement journal. `classify`
  counts NOMEM, IOERR and FULL as `kindLocal`. INTERRUPT can only come from the
  progress handler, because nothing calls `sqlite3_interrupt`. BUSY, CORRUPT,
  TOOBIG and LOCKED never roll back automatically.
  2. **The builder's claim holds.** `StepContext` runs in only two places: the
  first step of `scanIndex` and the `unread` count (`index.go` 934/939/958).
  Everything else on the cache connection steps or executes without a context.
  That covers gets, exists, upsert, insert, delete, keysets, lookups, the commit
  spool's scan (`WithoutCancel`), the commit, replay, and the checkpoints. In
  `Tx`, every one of those errors goes through `tx.fail`. In `ReadTx` they go
  through `cacheError`. The engine never touches the binding.
  3. **Read transactions.** After any stop, `ReadTx.check` refuses with
  `stoppedError`, and a scan already running is aborted row by row. So no later
  scan can see a commit made after the read began.
  4. **Savepoints.** `RollbackTo` runs only on a transaction that has not
  failed. A rolled-back transaction always fails it, either through `fail` or
  `errTxEnded`, so there is never a rollback to a savepoint that is gone.
  5. **Close path (#182).** `errTxEnded` is wrapped in `ErrLocal`, so it sets
  `failedLocally` and the close skips the checkpoint.
  6. **Unwanted stops.** `harmless` runs only while the scan holds a use of the
  connection. The lease task and `DB.Close` cannot roll the transaction back or
  close the connection in that window. `Rollback`, `Commit` or `Close` called
  inside the loop body sets `done` before the next step. So I found no case
  where the instance stops when it should not, and no case where `Autocommit`
  panics on a closed connection.
  7. **The binding's new rule is right by the source.** A failed or finished
  step reaches the last progress check in `VDBE_HALT_STATE`, so
  `sqlite3_stmt_busy` is false and the handler leaves the result alone. A step
  that has a row is still in run state and is still stopped. No step now runs on
  unstoppably: while a step does work in the virtual machine, the statement is
  busy. The browser build has `Autocommit` (the wasm build exports
  `sqlite3_get_autocommit`), and its `StepContext` still never stops a step, as
  before.

  **Planted faults.** Every one was caught by a short test, and the worktree is
  clean again:
  - Removing the `stmt_busy` condition failed `TestStmt_StepContext`. The core's
  tests still pass then, because `harmless` catches the lost transaction
  anyway.
  - `harmless` always returning true failed `TestScanRange` and
  `TestScanIndex_interrupt`.
  - Skipping `harmless` in `ReadTx.scan` only failed both read variants of
  `TestScanIndex_interrupt`.
  - `Autocommit` always false failed `TestConn_Autocommit` and
  `TestScanIndex_interrupt`. Always true failed `TestScanIndex_interrupt`.
  - `errTxEnded` not stopping the instance failed `TestScanIndex_interrupt`,
  `TestScanIndex_context`, `TestScanRange` and `TestScanIndex_order`.
  - Extra fault: dropping the `left == 0` check failed the write-rollback
  subtest of `TestStmt_StepContext`.
  - Baseline: the core under `-short` passes, and the new tests pass under
  `-race` with `-count=3`.

  **agy** (Gemini 4 Neon high, one run) reported no findings on the invariant or
  on unwanted stops. It made two minor claims, both checked:
  - If `StepContext` were used on a COMMIT that hits SQLITE_BUSY, it would give
  the context's error instead of BUSY. True: `sqlite3VdbeHalt` returns BUSY
  early (line 90908) while the statement is still busy. It has no consequence,
  because the core calls `StepContext` only on reads. At most it is a wording
  gap in the comment of `StepContext`.
  - The comment `// memory database for a test, ...` is detached from `memory`
  in `internal/sqlite/sqlite_test.go` (line 2332), because `failedContext` was
  inserted below it. True, cosmetic, worth a one-line fix.

  **codex** (gpt-6.2-sol, medium) declined: "flagged for possible cybersecurity
  risk", after about 44k tokens. As instructed, I did not retry or rephrase.

  **Sound:** the rule in the binding, the `Autocommit` check in the core, and
  the tests together close the hole everywhere I could reach.

  **Open:** what the diary already notes, that the simulation cannot produce
  this fault.

  **Tokens, roughly:** mine about 150k; agy 325k total plus 3.5M cache reads;
  codex 44k.

  The scratch directory `/Volumes/990/s4ql/step-context-review/` is deleted, and
  I pushed nothing.

› Message from general-purpose
  Not real. I added no test and committed nothing. Branch `s3store-ended` is at
  `origin/main` with nothing new on it, and I left no scratch files.

  1. **The contract rules it out.** `internal/store/store.go` says only
  ErrNotFound, ErrExists, ErrChanged, ErrChecksum and ErrTooLarge mean "not
  applied", and every other error of a write means its outcome is unknown. Since
  #187, `ended` (`internal/store/s3store/s3store.go`:408-421) returns
  `textOf{text, err: ctx.Err()}`, which matches only context.Canceled or
  context.DeadlineExceeded. Dropping the request's error can only remove a match
  with one of the five errors, never add one.
  2. **The core follows the contract.** On the manifest swap, `refusal`
  (`internal/core/lease.go`:100-102) checks only for those errors. Anything else
  sets `f.unknown`, and `publish` (`internal/core/tx.go`:1593-1612) returns
  `unknown`, which `Commit` reports as ErrOutcomeUnknown and stops serving until
  it knows. On the upload of the commit object (`tx.go`:1505-1544), any other
  error is retried, and an earlier attempt that landed is caught by ErrExists.
  An upload that ends with the context gives "not committed", which is true,
  because the manifest swap was never sent.
  3. **`put` adds nothing that could mislead.** It reaches a refusal only from a
  real status and code of an answer to a single attempt (`r.refusal`, 451-474,
  which gives no refusal when there was more than one attempt). Every other
  failure goes through `unknown(ended(...))` (385, 393), which wraps only a
  context error.
  4. **#187 made this tighter.** Before it, `ended` also wrapped the request's
  error, but `put` already passed it through `unknown`, so writes could not leak
  those errors then either. The one hole was in `r.failed`: it could take a 412
  status and code from a cause of the context's end that wrapped an SDK
  response error, and turn that into ErrExists or ErrChanged. #187 closed it
  with the new check at 566-569.
  5. **Simulation's store: same answer, it cannot report "not applied" for a
  write it applied.** It returns those errors only from `apply`
  (`internal/store/simstore/client.go`:443-454), as true refusals. A caller that
  gives up gets `simstore: request N: %w` around the sim's `waitErr`, which is
  only Canceled or DeadlineExceeded, while the carrier may still apply the
  write. Lost responses, and requests that were "never applied", get ErrFault
  errors that match none of the five. A request whose context ended before it
  was sent is never sent and gets the context's error, which counts as unknown.
  That is conservative, never wrong.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)
⏺ Agent "Check s3store ended for lost outcome" finished · 1m 32s

⏺ The suspicion about the S3 store is not real.

  The store's contract says only five named errors mean "not applied"; every
  other error of a write means its outcome is unknown, and the core follows that
  on both the manifest swap and the upload. Returning only the context's error
  can therefore never turn an unknown outcome into "not applied"; it can only
  remove a match. #187 made this tighter, not looser. The simulation's store
  holds the same way: it cannot report "not applied" for a write it applied.

⏺ The real-bucket failure is not a test problem, and it leads to a design
  question for you. It matters more than the planner question, so that one
  waits.

  What happened: Tigris answered a conditional create with 412 "exists",
  although it had applied it: the object at the key was that create's own write.
  The store made exactly one attempt. Our client retries nothing here (the SDK
  runs without a retryer, and Go resends a PUT only if no byte was sent).
  Whether Tigris retried internally or just answered wrongly cannot be told from
  our side.

  Why it matters: our code believes a 412. The agent wrote a failing test that
  shows the consequence at main:

  - Commit objects are safe: an "exists" on upload already counts as success.
  - The manifest swap is not: if the service applies a swap and answers 412, the
    instance concludes it lost its lease, and Commit returns "not committed",
    while the commit is the head of the log. That is a durable commit reported
    as failed.

  We have seen Tigris do this for a create, once. For a swap it is not shown,
  but nothing rules it out.

  The options:

  - (a) Never conclude "not applied" from a refusal alone. After a refused swap,
    the core reads the manifest back; if it carries this instance's own lease
    salt and head, the swap was applied and the commit succeeded. One extra
    read, only on a refusal, which is rare.
  - (b) Treat every 412 as "outcome unknown" in the store. Correct, but every
    ordinary lost race then goes through the unknown-outcome path and stops
    serving until resolved.
  - (c) Keep trusting a 412 as S3 documents it, and treat this as a Tigris bug.

  I recommend (a): it holds whatever the cause, and costs nothing on the normal
  path.

  Separately, it would be worth asking Tigris whether a conditional PUT can be
  answered 412 after being applied. That is contact with an outside party, so it
  is yours to do or to approve.

  The failing test is kept on the branch real-bucket-create. I wait for your
  choice between (a) read back after a refusal, (b) every 412 is unknown, or (c)
  trust a 412.

› Message from fabrik:builder
  The three faults are fixed on branch `core-hardening`, and so are all five
  hardening items. Last commit is 0da08f1c; `main` (ef06f290) is merged in and
  nothing is pushed. Lint, `make test`, and the core and engine packages without
  `-short` pass, and the new tests pass with `-race -count=20`.

  - **Fault 2, takeover on a shared cache.** Cause: a moved cache counted as a
  broken log, but since the catch-up hands replay each commit object after the
  one before it, a cache not at the parent can only have moved. Fix: replay
  returns `errCacheMoved` and the catch-up restarts from the cache's position,
  trusted only if the walk from the head finds it, else `ErrCacheNotInLog`. The
  old holder writes only its own commit, under SQLite's write lock from `Begin`,
  so nothing changed there. Proof: `TestDB_catchUp` (the review's scenario,
  fails with the old code) and `TestDB_catchUp_moved` (restart mid-log, and
  not-in-log). The simulation cannot reach it: each node has its own cache.
  - **Fault 3, replaced cache.** Cause: a reader served whatever file was at the
  path. Fix: the instance tracks the position its write connection committed or
  is committing (`cachePosition`, set by `DB.moving`/`DB.moved`); a read whose
  position fits neither of its two checks stops the instance as a local failure,
  and re-reads only after more than one commit between them. Proof: subtests of
  `TestReader_Read`. The simulation cannot delete a running cache; planted bug
  `fixedOnce` (compare with the second check only) was found in 977 of 1000
  seeds, so the clean runs show no false stops.
  - **Fault 4, overflow in the lease wait.** Cause: `Options.Watch` had no upper
  bound and `left + margin` overflowed. Fix: `Watch` is at most a year, and
  `watchWait` compares before it adds. `Lease.Duration` is the only manifest
  value the core computes with. Proof: `TestLease_duration` — the Open refusal
  and the `WatchWait` table fail without the fix; the year-long scenario passed
  before the fix too. Not reachable by the simulation.
  - **Hardening:**
    - An upload that finds an object already at its key reads it again without
  re-uploading, and fails as not committed after ten mismatching reads. One
  mismatch can't be the bound because the simulation damages up to 1 in 5
  downloads.
    - `Open` refuses a non-UTF-8 cache, and a cache at the empty log that holds
  tables; it re-reads the position first so a concurrent first commit on a
  shared cache isn't refused.
    - Replay enforces the 250-column key limit.
    - `Tx.scan` checks for a stop between rows.
    - `docs/todo/s3-client.md` is corrected.
  - **Fault 1:** its test was dropped here and stays on `adversarial-core`; the
  todo says so.
  - **Night-sized runs:**
    - Core planted bugs over 1000 seeds: all bugs pass, with no clean or control
  finds.
    - Engine planted bugs over 1000 seeds: passed at f2068419 except `undoInTx`,
  which was then fixed and rerun after the last merge with `errorSkipsRow` —
  not the full engine set again.
    - Engine `TestDifferential -sequences 400` and `TestSimulation` over 100
  seeds: pass.
  - **Test changes the shifted traces forced:**
    - Widened symptom lists for `errorSkipsRow` (engine, seed 22797), `undoInTx`
  (engine, seed 27822), `skipUnreadable` (core, seed 177) and
  `replayCommitsEarly` (core). Each seed was replayed with and without its bug
  and finds nothing without it.
    - The engine's `TestSimulation.kinds` floor for "statements with a subquery
  of another type than its column, refused" went from 86 to 82, moved by the

  Two decisions for you, the first one matters more:

  1. Tigris answered a conditional create with 412 "exists" although it had 
     applied it. We saw this once in CI. If it does the same to a manifest swap,
     Commit reports "not committed" for a commit that is durable; a failing
     test shows that. Options: (a) after a refusal, read the manifest back and
     accept it if it is our own; (b) treat every 412 as "outcome unknown"; (c)
     trust a 412. I recommend (a).
  2. Does s4ql copy SQLite's cost-based planner? It decides which of two errors
     an update gives in five measured cases, and whether an over-long text that
     no row needs is ever computed. (a) No, with each difference recorded and
     checked; (b) yes. I recommend (a).

❯ /fabrik:one-at-a-time                                                         

⏺ Decision 1: Tigris answered a write it had applied with "refused".

  In CI, a conditional create against the real bucket came back as 412 "an
  object exists", but the object at the key was the one that very create had
  written. We made one attempt; nothing on our side retried.

  s4ql believes a 412. If Tigris does the same to a manifest swap, Commit
  returns "not committed" for a commit that is durable. A failing test shows
  that path.

  My recommendation: after a refusal, read the manifest back, and if it carries
  this instance's own lease and head, count the swap as applied. It costs one
  extra read, only on a refusal.

  Do you agree?
❯ Hmm. That's a tough one. Are we sure our observation is correct? I know       
  Tigris builds on FoundationDB underneath, which is heavily tested and vetted, 
  so I would suspect our own code is at fault unless there is strong evidence   
  to the contrary.                                                              

⏺ No, we are not sure. The evidence is thin, and your prior is reasonable.

  What we have is one CI log line plus reasoning from our code that no retry
  exists on our side. What we lack is anything from the wire: no request ID, no
  record of which protocol was used or how many times the request was actually
  sent. The investigator concluded "Tigris" by ruling out our client, and that
  ruling-out is the weak part. For example, it checked Go's HTTP/1 rules for
  resending a PUT, but I don't know that it checked HTTP/2, where the transport
  can resend a request the server says it did not process, and where our way of
  counting attempts may not see it.

  So I'd treat it as "unexplained" and get evidence before deciding anything:

  A builder adds wire-level tracing to the real-bucket test (request ID,
  protocol, every send as the transport sees it), and a small on-demand CI job
  repeats conditional creates on unique keys a few thousand times, reading each
  key back. If it happens again, we will know whether one request or two went
  out, and have something concrete either to fix in our code or to show Tigris.

  Shall I start that?

  and not creating too many events/spans all the time. (But before you do that, 
  maybe time for export and compaction?)
